========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, mostly it’s the sleeping, doctor. I just don’t sleep right anymore. I’ll go to bed and I’m tired, I know I’m tired, but then I just lay there for what feels like a long time, staring off and waiting for sleep to come. And then when I finally do drift off, it doesn’t feel like good sleep.

I wake up from bad dreams sometimes, or even just little noises — things that maybe shouldn’t bother a person that much — and once I’m awake, that’s it for a while. I can’t seem to settle myself back down. I’ll turn this way and that, try to get comfortable, try to tell myself it’s nothing, but my body just won’t listen. So that’s why I came in, really. It’s been wearing on me.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I do. Maybe not worry in the way I used to think of worrying, like about bills or appointments or whether I left something on the stove. It’s more like… I don’t trust ordinary things the same anymore.

I know it probably sounds foolish when I say it out loud, but during the day I’ll catch myself thinking that everyday things aren’t really safe. Simple things that other people don’t think twice about, I start feeling like if I let my guard down or trust them, they’ll turn on me somehow. And I can tell myself, “That doesn’t make sense,” but it still sits there in my mind.

It’s tiring, because it makes the day feel… uneasy, I guess. Like I’m always waiting for something ordinary to become a problem.

I’ll go to bed and I’m tired, I know I’m tired, but then I just lay there for what feels like a long time, staring off and waiting for sleep to come. Simple things that other people don’t think twice about, I start feeling like if I let my guard down or trust them, they’ll turn on me somehow. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think so. It’s hard for me to separate what’s in my body from what’s in my head, if that makes sense. When I’m uneasy, I don’t just think about things — I start checking them.

Like if I go somewhere now, even a regular place, I catch myself looking around before I can relax. I look up at things, wondering what might fall. I look for the exits, where I’d go if I had to get out. And I pay attention to the floor, or the steps, whether they look steady, whether there’s something loose or uneven there.

I don’t always mean to do it. It’s just like my body won’t let me sit easy until I’ve looked everything over. Then maybe I can settle a little, but even then I’m still sort of watching.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yes, I think I do. I don’t always call it avoiding, because I’ll make it sound more reasonable in my own head, you know? I’ll tell myself I’m just tired, or the weather’s not right, or I’ve got something else to do around the house.

But I keep finding reasons not to go down to the senior center. I used to go more, just to be around people a bit, sit and talk, maybe have coffee. Now I’ll get ready like I might go, and then I start thinking about it too much, and pretty soon I’ve talked myself out of it.

And the garage is another one. I don’t like being out there the way I used to. Especially around the tools… and over near where I used to keep that ladder. I’ll say, “Well, that can wait,” or “I don’t need to fool with that today,” but truthfully, I just don’t want to be near it. I can feel myself getting uneasy before I even get started, so I just shut the door and leave it alone.

I can feel myself getting uneasy before I even get started, so I just shut the door and leave it alone.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever feel like you have to repeat certain actions or rituals to help you feel safer or less uneasy?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 74 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, maybe.

I mean, I’ll check things, like I said before, and sometimes I might look at something more than once because I don’t quite believe myself the first time. But I don’t have, like, a set thing I do over and over, if that’s what you’re asking. No counting or touching things a certain number of times, nothing like that.

It’s more that I hesitate. I’ll stand there longer than I probably need to, looking something over, trying to feel sure. But I don’t know if I’d call that a ritual. It doesn’t feel organized like that. It’s just me trying to settle myself down, I guess, and sometimes it works a little, sometimes it doesn’t.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… there are. I guess the two that come to mind right away are the senior center and my garage.

The senior center sounds harmless when I say it out loud, and I know that. It’s just a place where people sit around, talk, have coffee, maybe play cards. But I keep finding reasons not to go. I’ll think, “Well, maybe tomorrow,” or “I don’t really need to be out today,” and then the day passes and I haven’t gone. It’s not that anybody there has done anything wrong. It’s more the thought of going there and being out somewhere… I start getting uneasy before I even leave.

And the garage, that one is more direct, I suppose. I don’t like being around the tools much anymore, and especially not over by the place where I used to keep the ladder. Even if I’m only going in there for something small, I notice myself staying away from that side. I’ll tell myself the work can wait, or I don’t have the right energy for it, but really I just don’t want to be near it. It sets me off inside some, so I shut the door and leave it alone.

I’ll go to bed and I’m tired, I know I’m tired, but then I just lay there for what feels like a long time, staring off and waiting for sleep to come. And then when I finally do drift off, it doesn’t feel like good sleep.\n\nI wake up from bad dreams sometimes, or even just little noises — things that maybe shouldn’t bother a person that much — and once I’m awake, that’s it for a while. I can’t seem to settle myself back down. I’ll turn this way and that, try to get comfortable, try to tell myself it’s nothing, but my body just won’t listen. So that’s why I came in, really. Like I’m always waiting for something ordinary to become a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical sensations, like pain or tension, when you're feeling uneasy or on guard?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s hard for me to separate what’s in my body from what’s in my head, if that makes sense. I look up at things, wondering what might fall. I look for the exits, where I’d go if I had to get out. And I pay attention to the floor, or the steps, whether they look steady, whether there’s something loose or uneven there.\n\nI don’t always mean to do it. It’s just like my body won’t let me sit easy until I’ve looked everything over. Then maybe I can settle a little, but even then I’m still sort of watching.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places or activities because of these feelings of unease?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think I do. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yes, that happens. Not all day every day, maybe, but when something sets it off, it’s hard to get it out of my head.

Like if I hear metal scrape against something — even something simple, like somebody dragging a tool or a chair leg catching wrong — it can just… it jumps me right back. Or if I’m going past a house and there’s a ladder leaning up in the yard, not even my ladder, just any ladder sitting there at the wrong angle, I’ll see it and then my mind goes right back to it.

It’s like for a second I’m under there again, waiting for help. I know I’m not, I know I’m standing right where I am, but my head doesn’t seem to care right away. The picture comes back, and then I keep thinking about it even after I try to push it aside. I’ll tell myself, “Quit it, that’s over, you’re fine,” but it keeps circling around for a while. Sometimes I have to really work to get my mind onto something else.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n7\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F45.3, F48.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, mostly it’s the sleeping, doctor. I just don’t sleep right anymore. I’ll go to bed and I’m tired, I know I’m tired, but then I just lay there for what feels like a long time, staring off and waiting for sleep to come. And then when I finally do drift off, it doesn’t feel like good sleep.\n\nI wake up from bad dreams sometimes, or even just little noises — things that maybe shouldn’t bother a person that much — and once I’m awake, that’s it for a while. I can’t seem to settle myself back down. I’ll turn this way and that, try to get comfortable, try to tell myself it’s nothing, but my body just won’t listen. So that’s why I came in, really. It’s been wearing on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things during the day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Maybe not worry in the way I used to think of worrying, like about bills or appointments or whether I left something on the stove. It’s more like… I don’t trust ordinary things the same anymore.\n\nI know it probably sounds foolish when I say it out loud, but during the day I’ll catch myself thinking that everyday things aren’t really safe. Simple things that other people don’t think twice about, I start feeling like if I let my guard down or trust them, they’ll turn on me somehow. And I can tell myself, “That doesn’t make sense,” but it still sits there in my mind.\n\nIt’s tiring, because it makes the day feel… uneasy, I guess. Like I’m always waiting for something ordinary to become a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical sensations, like pain or tension, when you're feeling uneasy or on guard?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s hard for me to separate what’s in my body from what’s in my head, if that makes sense. When I’m uneasy, I don’t just think about things — I start checking them.\n\nLike if I go somewhere now, even a regular place, I catch myself looking around before I can relax. I look up at things, wondering what might fall. I look for the exits, where I’d go if I had to get out. And I pay attention to the floor, or the steps, whether they look steady, whether there’s something loose or uneven there.\n\nI don’t always mean to do it. It’s just like my body won’t let me sit easy until I’ve looked everything over. Then maybe I can settle a little, but even then I’m still sort of watching.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places or activities because of these feelings of unease?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think I do. I don’t always call it avoiding, because I’ll make it sound more reasonable in my own head, you know? I’ll tell myself I’m just tired, or the weather’s not right, or I’ve got something else to do around the house.\n\nBut I keep finding reasons not to go down to the senior center. I used to go more, just to be around people a bit, sit and talk, maybe have coffee. Now I’ll get ready like I might go, and then I start thinking about it too much, and pretty soon I’ve talked myself out of it.\n\nAnd the garage is another one. I don’t like being out there the way I used to. Especially around the tools… and over near where I used to keep that ladder. I’ll say, “Well, that can wait,” or “I don’t need to fool with that today,” but truthfully, I just don’t want to be near it. I can feel myself getting uneasy before I even get started, so I just shut the door and leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to repeat certain actions or rituals to help you feel safer or less uneasy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, maybe.\n\nI mean, I’ll check things, like I said before, and sometimes I might look at something more than once because I don’t quite believe myself the first time. But I don’t have, like, a set thing I do over and over, if that’s what you’re asking. No counting or touching things a certain number of times, nothing like that.\n\nIt’s more that I hesitate. I’ll stand there longer than I probably need to, looking something over, trying to feel sure. But I don’t know if I’d call that a ritual. It doesn’t feel organized like that. It’s just me trying to settle myself down, I guess, and sometimes it works a little, sometimes it doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific objects or situations that consistently make you feel uneasy?\n\nPatient: Yes… there are. I guess the two that come to mind right away are the senior center and my garage.\n\nThe senior center sounds harmless when I say it out loud, and I know that. It’s just a place where people sit around, talk, have coffee, maybe play cards. But I keep finding reasons not to go. I’ll think, “Well, maybe tomorrow,” or “I don’t really need to be out today,” and then the day passes and I haven’t gone. It’s not that anybody there has done anything wrong. It’s more the thought of going there and being out somewhere… I start getting uneasy before I even leave.\n\nAnd the garage, that one is more direct, I suppose. I don’t like being around the tools much anymore, and especially not over by the place where I used to keep the ladder. Even if I’m only going in there for something small, I notice myself staying away from that side. I’ll tell myself the work can wait, or I don’t have the right energy for it, but really I just don’t want to be near it. It sets me off inside some, so I shut the door and leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that repeat over and over in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that happens. Not all day every day, maybe, but when something sets it off, it’s hard to get it out of my head.\n\nLike if I hear metal scrape against something — even something simple, like somebody dragging a tool or a chair leg catching wrong — it can just… it jumps me right back. Or if I’m going past a house and there’s a ladder leaning up in the yard, not even my ladder, just any ladder sitting there at the wrong angle, I’ll see it and then my mind goes right back to it.\n\nIt’s like for a second I’m under there again, waiting for help. I know I’m not, I know I’m standing right where I am, but my head doesn’t seem to care right away. The picture comes back, and then I keep thinking about it even after I try to push it aside. I’ll tell myself, “Quit it, that’s over, you’re fine,” but it keeps circling around for a while. Sometimes I have to really work to get my mind onto something else.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… that’s a good way to put it, I think. It’s not like I sit down and decide to think about it. I’ll be doing something ordinary, or just going along, and then if I hear metal scrape — like a tool dragging or something catching that sharp way — it just pushes right in.

Or if I see a ladder leaning in somebody’s yard, even if it’s not mine and I don’t know the person, my mind doesn’t just notice it and move on. The whole thing comes back in my head. For a moment it’s like I’m under there again, waiting for help, and I don’t want to be thinking about that at all.

I try to tell myself, “You’re not there now,” but the thought kind of forces its way in before I can stop it. It’s not constant, but when it happens, it really does feel like it gets into my head without permission.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F45.3, F48.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, mostly it’s the sleeping, doctor. I just don’t sleep right anymore. I’ll go to bed and I’m tired, I know I’m tired, but then I just lay there for what feels like a long time, staring off and waiting for sleep to come. And then when I finally do drift off, it doesn’t feel like good sleep.\n\nI wake up from bad dreams sometimes, or even just little noises — things that maybe shouldn’t bother a person that much — and once I’m awake, that’s it for a while. I can’t seem to settle myself back down. I’ll turn this way and that, try to get comfortable, try to tell myself it’s nothing, but my body just won’t listen. So that’s why I came in, really. It’s been wearing on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things during the day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Maybe not worry in the way I used to think of worrying, like about bills or appointments or whether I left something on the stove. It’s more like… I don’t trust ordinary things the same anymore.\n\nI know it probably sounds foolish when I say it out loud, but during the day I’ll catch myself thinking that everyday things aren’t really safe. Simple things that other people don’t think twice about, I start feeling like if I let my guard down or trust them, they’ll turn on me somehow. And I can tell myself, “That doesn’t make sense,” but it still sits there in my mind.\n\nIt’s tiring, because it makes the day feel… uneasy, I guess. Like I’m always waiting for something ordinary to become a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical sensations, like pain or tension, when you're feeling uneasy or on guard?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s hard for me to separate what’s in my body from what’s in my head, if that makes sense. When I’m uneasy, I don’t just think about things — I start checking them.\n\nLike if I go somewhere now, even a regular place, I catch myself looking around before I can relax. I look up at things, wondering what might fall. I look for the exits, where I’d go if I had to get out. And I pay attention to the floor, or the steps, whether they look steady, whether there’s something loose or uneven there.\n\nI don’t always mean to do it. It’s just like my body won’t let me sit easy until I’ve looked everything over. Then maybe I can settle a little, but even then I’m still sort of watching.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places or activities because of these feelings of unease?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think I do. I don’t always call it avoiding, because I’ll make it sound more reasonable in my own head, you know? I’ll tell myself I’m just tired, or the weather’s not right, or I’ve got something else to do around the house.\n\nBut I keep finding reasons not to go down to the senior center. I used to go more, just to be around people a bit, sit and talk, maybe have coffee. Now I’ll get ready like I might go, and then I start thinking about it too much, and pretty soon I’ve talked myself out of it.\n\nAnd the garage is another one. I don’t like being out there the way I used to. Especially around the tools… and over near where I used to keep that ladder. I’ll say, “Well, that can wait,” or “I don’t need to fool with that today,” but truthfully, I just don’t want to be near it. I can feel myself getting uneasy before I even get started, so I just shut the door and leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to repeat certain actions or rituals to help you feel safer or less uneasy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, maybe.\n\nI mean, I’ll check things, like I said before, and sometimes I might look at something more than once because I don’t quite believe myself the first time. But I don’t have, like, a set thing I do over and over, if that’s what you’re asking. No counting or touching things a certain number of times, nothing like that.\n\nIt’s more that I hesitate. I’ll stand there longer than I probably need to, looking something over, trying to feel sure. But I don’t know if I’d call that a ritual. It doesn’t feel organized like that. It’s just me trying to settle myself down, I guess, and sometimes it works a little, sometimes it doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific objects or situations that consistently make you feel uneasy?\n\nPatient: Yes… there are. I guess the two that come to mind right away are the senior center and my garage.\n\nThe senior center sounds harmless when I say it out loud, and I know that. It’s just a place where people sit around, talk, have coffee, maybe play cards. But I keep finding reasons not to go. I’ll think, “Well, maybe tomorrow,” or “I don’t really need to be out today,” and then the day passes and I haven’t gone. It’s not that anybody there has done anything wrong. It’s more the thought of going there and being out somewhere… I start getting uneasy before I even leave.\n\nAnd the garage, that one is more direct, I suppose. I don’t like being around the tools much anymore, and especially not over by the place where I used to keep the ladder. Even if I’m only going in there for something small, I notice myself staying away from that side. I’ll tell myself the work can wait, or I don’t have the right energy for it, but really I just don’t want to be near it. It sets me off inside some, so I shut the door and leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that repeat over and over in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that happens. Not all day every day, maybe, but when something sets it off, it’s hard to get it out of my head.\n\nLike if I hear metal scrape against something — even something simple, like somebody dragging a tool or a chair leg catching wrong — it can just… it jumps me right back. Or if I’m going past a house and there’s a ladder leaning up in the yard, not even my ladder, just any ladder sitting there at the wrong angle, I’ll see it and then my mind goes right back to it.\n\nIt’s like for a second I’m under there again, waiting for help. I know I’m not, I know I’m standing right where I am, but my head doesn’t seem to care right away. The picture comes back, and then I keep thinking about it even after I try to push it aside. I’ll tell myself, “Quit it, that’s over, you’re fine,” but it keeps circling around for a while. Sometimes I have to really work to get my mind onto something else.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that feel like they're forcing their way into your mind, even when you don't want to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s a good way to put it, I think. It’s not like I sit down and decide to think about it. I’ll be doing something ordinary, or just going along, and then if I hear metal scrape — like a tool dragging or something catching that sharp way — it just pushes right in.\n\nOr if I see a ladder leaning in somebody’s yard, even if it’s not mine and I don’t know the person, my mind doesn’t just notice it and move on. The whole thing comes back in my head. For a moment it’s like I’m under there again, waiting for help, and I don’t want to be thinking about that at all.\n\nI try to tell myself, “You’re not there now,” but the thought kind of forces its way in before I can stop it. It’s not constant, but when it happens, it really does feel like it gets into my head without permission.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Not all day every day, maybe, but when something sets it off, it’s hard to get it out of my head.\n\nLike if I hear metal scrape against something — even something simple, like somebody dragging a tool or a chair leg catching wrong — it can just… it jumps me right back. Or if I’m going past a house and there’s a ladder leaning up in the yard, not even my ladder, just any ladder sitting there at the wrong angle, I’ll see it and then my mind goes right back to it.\n\nIt’s like for a second I’m under there again, waiting for help. I know I’m not, I know I’m standing right where I am, but my head doesn’t seem to care right away. The picture comes back, and then I keep thinking about it even after I try to push it aside. I’ll tell myself, “Quit it, that’s over, you’re fine,” but it keeps circling around for a while. Sometimes I have to really work to get my mind onto something else.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that feel like they're forcing their way into your mind, even when you don't want to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s a good way to put it, I think. It’s not like I sit down and decide to think about it. I’ll be doing something ordinary, or just going along, and then if I hear metal scrape — like a tool dragging or something catching that sharp way — it just pushes right in.\n\nOr if I see a ladder leaning in somebody’s yard, even if it’s not mine and I don’t know the person, my mind doesn’t just notice it and move on. The whole thing comes back in my head. For a moment it’s like I’m under there again, waiting for help, and I don’t want to be thinking about that at all.\n\nI try to tell myself, “You’re not there now,” but the thought kind of forces its way in before I can stop it. It’s not constant, but when it happens, it really does feel like it gets into my head without permission.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like these thoughts that come into your mind are a part of you or do they feel like they are coming from outside of yourself?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 74 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Intrusive_Symptoms\nmanifestation: \"Sometimes when I hear metal scrape or see a ladder leaning in somebody's yard, the whole thing jumps back in my head like I'm under there again waiting for help.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No… I wouldn’t say it feels like it’s coming from outside me, like somebody else is putting it there. It’s in my own head, I know that.

But it doesn’t feel like I’m choosing it, either. That’s the part that bothers me. I’ll hear that metal scraping sound, or I’ll see a ladder leaning up in somebody’s yard, and before I even have time to think, the whole thing jumps back in my mind. It’s like I’m right back under there again, waiting for help.

So… it’s mine, I guess, but it doesn’t feel under my control. It just comes rushing in.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, mostly it’s the sleeping, doctor. I just don’t sleep right anymore. I’ll go to bed and I’m tired, I know I’m tired, but then I just lay there for what feels like a long time, staring off and waiting for sleep to come. And then when I finally do drift off, it doesn’t feel like good sleep.\n\nI wake up from bad dreams sometimes, or even just little noises — things that maybe shouldn’t bother a person that much — and once I’m awake, that’s it for a while. I can’t seem to settle myself back down. I’ll turn this way and that, try to get comfortable, try to tell myself it’s nothing, but my body just won’t listen. So that’s why I came in, really. It’s been wearing on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things during the day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Maybe not worry in the way I used to think of worrying, like about bills or appointments or whether I left something on the stove. It’s more like… I don’t trust ordinary things the same anymore.\n\nI know it probably sounds foolish when I say it out loud, but during the day I’ll catch myself thinking that everyday things aren’t really safe. Simple things that other people don’t think twice about, I start feeling like if I let my guard down or trust them, they’ll turn on me somehow. And I can tell myself, “That doesn’t make sense,” but it still sits there in my mind.\n\nIt’s tiring, because it makes the day feel… uneasy, I guess. Like I’m always waiting for something ordinary to become a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical sensations, like pain or tension, when you're feeling uneasy or on guard?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s hard for me to separate what’s in my body from what’s in my head, if that makes sense. When I’m uneasy, I don’t just think about things — I start checking them.\n\nLike if I go somewhere now, even a regular place, I catch myself looking around before I can relax. I look up at things, wondering what might fall. I look for the exits, where I’d go if I had to get out. And I pay attention to the floor, or the steps, whether they look steady, whether there’s something loose or uneven there.\n\nI don’t always mean to do it. It’s just like my body won’t let me sit easy until I’ve looked everything over. Then maybe I can settle a little, but even then I’m still sort of watching.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places or activities because of these feelings of unease?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think I do. I don’t always call it avoiding, because I’ll make it sound more reasonable in my own head, you know? I’ll tell myself I’m just tired, or the weather’s not right, or I’ve got something else to do around the house.\n\nBut I keep finding reasons not to go down to the senior center. I used to go more, just to be around people a bit, sit and talk, maybe have coffee. Now I’ll get ready like I might go, and then I start thinking about it too much, and pretty soon I’ve talked myself out of it.\n\nAnd the garage is another one. I don’t like being out there the way I used to. Especially around the tools… and over near where I used to keep that ladder. I’ll say, “Well, that can wait,” or “I don’t need to fool with that today,” but truthfully, I just don’t want to be near it. I can feel myself getting uneasy before I even get started, so I just shut the door and leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to repeat certain actions or rituals to help you feel safer or less uneasy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, maybe.\n\nI mean, I’ll check things, like I said before, and sometimes I might look at something more than once because I don’t quite believe myself the first time. But I don’t have, like, a set thing I do over and over, if that’s what you’re asking. No counting or touching things a certain number of times, nothing like that.\n\nIt’s more that I hesitate. I’ll stand there longer than I probably need to, looking something over, trying to feel sure. But I don’t know if I’d call that a ritual. It doesn’t feel organized like that. It’s just me trying to settle myself down, I guess, and sometimes it works a little, sometimes it doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific objects or situations that consistently make you feel uneasy?\n\nPatient: Yes… there are. I guess the two that come to mind right away are the senior center and my garage.\n\nThe senior center sounds harmless when I say it out loud, and I know that. It’s just a place where people sit around, talk, have coffee, maybe play cards. But I keep finding reasons not to go. I’ll think, “Well, maybe tomorrow,” or “I don’t really need to be out today,” and then the day passes and I haven’t gone. It’s not that anybody there has done anything wrong. It’s more the thought of going there and being out somewhere… I start getting uneasy before I even leave.\n\nAnd the garage, that one is more direct, I suppose. I don’t like being around the tools much anymore, and especially not over by the place where I used to keep the ladder. Even if I’m only going in there for something small, I notice myself staying away from that side. I’ll tell myself the work can wait, or I don’t have the right energy for it, but really I just don’t want to be near it. It sets me off inside some, so I shut the door and leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that repeat over and over in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that happens. Not all day every day, maybe, but when something sets it off, it’s hard to get it out of my head.\n\nLike if I hear metal scrape against something — even something simple, like somebody dragging a tool or a chair leg catching wrong — it can just… it jumps me right back. Or if I’m going past a house and there’s a ladder leaning up in the yard, not even my ladder, just any ladder sitting there at the wrong angle, I’ll see it and then my mind goes right back to it.\n\nIt’s like for a second I’m under there again, waiting for help. I know I’m not, I know I’m standing right where I am, but my head doesn’t seem to care right away. The picture comes back, and then I keep thinking about it even after I try to push it aside. I’ll tell myself, “Quit it, that’s over, you’re fine,” but it keeps circling around for a while. Sometimes I have to really work to get my mind onto something else.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that feel like they're forcing their way into your mind, even when you don't want to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s a good way to put it, I think. It’s not like I sit down and decide to think about it. Sometimes I have to really work to get my mind onto something else.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that feel like they're forcing their way into your mind, even when you don't want to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s a good way to put it, I think. It’s not like I sit down and decide to think about it. I’ll be doing something ordinary, or just going along, and then if I hear metal scrape — like a tool dragging or something catching that sharp way — it just pushes right in.\n\nOr if I see a ladder leaning in somebody’s yard, even if it’s not mine and I don’t know the person, my mind doesn’t just notice it and move on. The whole thing comes back in my head. For a moment it’s like I’m under there again, waiting for help, and I don’t want to be thinking about that at all.\n\nI try to tell myself, “You’re not there now,” but the thought kind of forces its way in before I can stop it. It’s not constant, but when it happens, it really does feel like it gets into my head without permission.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts that come into your mind are a part of you or do they feel like they are coming from outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: No… I wouldn’t say it feels like it’s coming from outside me, like somebody else is putting it there. It’s in my own head, I know that.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel like I’m choosing it, either. That’s the part that bothers me. I’ll hear that metal scraping sound, or I’ll see a ladder leaning up in somebody’s yard, and before I even have time to think, the whole thing jumps back in my mind. It’s like I’m right back under there again, waiting for help.\n\nSo… it’s mine, I guess, but it doesn’t feel under my control. It just comes rushing in.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed that your unease or these repeating thoughts are affecting your ability to work or interact with family and friends?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… some. I mean, I’m not falling apart completely or anything. I still get up, wash up, eat, do the basic things I need to do. I can get through the day, mostly. But my day is a lot smaller than it used to be, if that makes sense.

I don’t go to the senior center anymore like I used to. I used to go just to be around people, maybe play cards, talk a little, have coffee. Now I find reasons not to go. And around the house, there are things I just avoid, more than I like to admit. My wife ends up doing chores I used to handle, and I feel bad about that. I’ll tell myself I’m going to do it, then I see something or I start thinking too much, and I just leave it.

And my bridge friends… I don’t visit with them much anymore either. I used to enjoy that. Now it feels easier to stay home, but then staying home makes everything feel even narrower. So yes, it has affected things. Not in a way where I can’t function at all, but enough that my wife notices, and I notice too.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F45.3, F48.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, mostly it’s the sleeping, doctor. I just don’t sleep right anymore. I’ll go to bed and I’m tired, I know I’m tired, but then I just lay there for what feels like a long time, staring off and waiting for sleep to come. And then when I finally do drift off, it doesn’t feel like good sleep.\n\nI wake up from bad dreams sometimes, or even just little noises — things that maybe shouldn’t bother a person that much — and once I’m awake, that’s it for a while. I can’t seem to settle myself back down. I’ll turn this way and that, try to get comfortable, try to tell myself it’s nothing, but my body just won’t listen. So that’s why I came in, really. It’s been wearing on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things during the day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Maybe not worry in the way I used to think of worrying, like about bills or appointments or whether I left something on the stove. It’s more like… I don’t trust ordinary things the same anymore.\n\nI know it probably sounds foolish when I say it out loud, but during the day I’ll catch myself thinking that everyday things aren’t really safe. Simple things that other people don’t think twice about, I start feeling like if I let my guard down or trust them, they’ll turn on me somehow. And I can tell myself, “That doesn’t make sense,” but it still sits there in my mind.\n\nIt’s tiring, because it makes the day feel… uneasy, I guess. Like I’m always waiting for something ordinary to become a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical sensations, like pain or tension, when you're feeling uneasy or on guard?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s hard for me to separate what’s in my body from what’s in my head, if that makes sense. When I’m uneasy, I don’t just think about things — I start checking them.\n\nLike if I go somewhere now, even a regular place, I catch myself looking around before I can relax. I look up at things, wondering what might fall. I look for the exits, where I’d go if I had to get out. And I pay attention to the floor, or the steps, whether they look steady, whether there’s something loose or uneven there.\n\nI don’t always mean to do it. It’s just like my body won’t let me sit easy until I’ve looked everything over. Then maybe I can settle a little, but even then I’m still sort of watching.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places or activities because of these feelings of unease?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think I do. I don’t always call it avoiding, because I’ll make it sound more reasonable in my own head, you know? I’ll tell myself I’m just tired, or the weather’s not right, or I’ve got something else to do around the house.\n\nBut I keep finding reasons not to go down to the senior center. I used to go more, just to be around people a bit, sit and talk, maybe have coffee. Now I’ll get ready like I might go, and then I start thinking about it too much, and pretty soon I’ve talked myself out of it.\n\nAnd the garage is another one. I don’t like being out there the way I used to. Especially around the tools… and over near where I used to keep that ladder. I’ll say, “Well, that can wait,” or “I don’t need to fool with that today,” but truthfully, I just don’t want to be near it. I can feel myself getting uneasy before I even get started, so I just shut the door and leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to repeat certain actions or rituals to help you feel safer or less uneasy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, maybe.\n\nI mean, I’ll check things, like I said before, and sometimes I might look at something more than once because I don’t quite believe myself the first time. But I don’t have, like, a set thing I do over and over, if that’s what you’re asking. No counting or touching things a certain number of times, nothing like that.\n\nIt’s more that I hesitate. I’ll stand there longer than I probably need to, looking something over, trying to feel sure. But I don’t know if I’d call that a ritual. It doesn’t feel organized like that. It’s just me trying to settle myself down, I guess, and sometimes it works a little, sometimes it doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific objects or situations that consistently make you feel uneasy?\n\nPatient: Yes… there are. I guess the two that come to mind right away are the senior center and my garage.\n\nThe senior center sounds harmless when I say it out loud, and I know that. It’s just a place where people sit around, talk, have coffee, maybe play cards. But I keep finding reasons not to go. I’ll think, “Well, maybe tomorrow,” or “I don’t really need to be out today,” and then the day passes and I haven’t gone. It’s not that anybody there has done anything wrong. It’s more the thought of going there and being out somewhere… I start getting uneasy before I even leave.\n\nAnd the garage, that one is more direct, I suppose. I don’t like being around the tools much anymore, and especially not over by the place where I used to keep the ladder. Even if I’m only going in there for something small, I notice myself staying away from that side. I’ll tell myself the work can wait, or I don’t have the right energy for it, but really I just don’t want to be near it. It sets me off inside some, so I shut the door and leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that repeat over and over in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that happens. Not all day every day, maybe, but when something sets it off, it’s hard to get it out of my head.\n\nLike if I hear metal scrape against something — even something simple, like somebody dragging a tool or a chair leg catching wrong — it can just… it jumps me right back. Or if I’m going past a house and there’s a ladder leaning up in the yard, not even my ladder, just any ladder sitting there at the wrong angle, I’ll see it and then my mind goes right back to it.\n\nIt’s like for a second I’m under there again, waiting for help. I know I’m not, I know I’m standing right where I am, but my head doesn’t seem to care right away. The picture comes back, and then I keep thinking about it even after I try to push it aside. I’ll tell myself, “Quit it, that’s over, you’re fine,” but it keeps circling around for a while. Sometimes I have to really work to get my mind onto something else.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that feel like they're forcing their way into your mind, even when you don't want to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s a good way to put it, I think. It’s not like I sit down and decide to think about it. I’ll be doing something ordinary, or just going along, and then if I hear metal scrape — like a tool dragging or something catching that sharp way — it just pushes right in.\n\nOr if I see a ladder leaning in somebody’s yard, even if it’s not mine and I don’t know the person, my mind doesn’t just notice it and move on. The whole thing comes back in my head. For a moment it’s like I’m under there again, waiting for help, and I don’t want to be thinking about that at all.\n\nI try to tell myself, “You’re not there now,” but the thought kind of forces its way in before I can stop it. It’s not constant, but when it happens, it really does feel like it gets into my head without permission.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts that come into your mind are a part of you or do they feel like they are coming from outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: No… I wouldn’t say it feels like it’s coming from outside me, like somebody else is putting it there. It’s in my own head, I know that.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel like I’m choosing it, either. That’s the part that bothers me. I’ll hear that metal scraping sound, or I’ll see a ladder leaning up in somebody’s yard, and before I even have time to think, the whole thing jumps back in my mind. It’s like I’m right back under there again, waiting for help.\n\nSo… it’s mine, I guess, but it doesn’t feel under my control. It just comes rushing in.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your unease or these repeating thoughts are affecting your ability to work or interact with family and friends?\n\nPatient: Yes… some. I mean, I’m not falling apart completely or anything. I still get up, wash up, eat, do the basic things I need to do. I can get through the day, mostly. But my day is a lot smaller than it used to be, if that makes sense.\n\nI don’t go to the senior center anymore like I used to. I used to go just to be around people, maybe play cards, talk a little, have coffee. Now I find reasons not to go. And around the house, there are things I just avoid, more than I like to admit. My wife ends up doing chores I used to handle, and I feel bad about that. I’ll tell myself I’m going to do it, then I see something or I start thinking too much, and I just leave it.\n\nAnd my bridge friends… I don’t visit with them much anymore either. I used to enjoy that. Now it feels easier to stay home, but then staying home makes everything feel even narrower. So yes, it has affected things. Not in a way where I can’t function at all, but enough that my wife notices, and I notice too.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yes, I’d say it has, though maybe not in some big dramatic way. I still do my basic things, you know — I get up, I wash, I eat, I can manage the ordinary routine. But with people… I’ve pulled back more than I meant to.

I don’t go to the senior center anymore, and I used to go there pretty regularly. It was a place to see folks, have coffee, sit and talk. Now I just don’t go. I tell myself I’m not in the mood, or I’ll go next week, but then next week comes and I still stay home.

And with my wife, it affects things around the house. I avoid certain things, or I put them off so long that she ends up doing chores I used to take care of. I don’t like that. It makes me feel like I’m leaving things on her, and that’s not how I want to be.

My bridge friends too… I don’t visit with them much anymore. I used to enjoy that, just sitting with them and playing and talking. Now my days have gotten smaller, I guess. More of my time is just at home, not seeing people the way I used to. So yes, it has affected my relationships some.

From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF41.1, F45.3, F48.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, mostly it’s the sleeping, doctor. I just don’t sleep right anymore. I’ll go to bed and I’m tired, I know I’m tired, but then I just lay there for what feels like a long time, staring off and waiting for sleep to come. And then when I finally do drift off, it doesn’t feel like good sleep.\n\nI wake up from bad dreams sometimes, or even just little noises — things that maybe shouldn’t bother a person that much — and once I’m awake, that’s it for a while. I can’t seem to settle myself back down. I’ll turn this way and that, try to get comfortable, try to tell myself it’s nothing, but my body just won’t listen. So that’s why I came in, really. It’s been wearing on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things during the day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Maybe not worry in the way I used to think of worrying, like about bills or appointments or whether I left something on the stove. It’s more like… I don’t trust ordinary things the same anymore.\n\nI know it probably sounds foolish when I say it out loud, but during the day I’ll catch myself thinking that everyday things aren’t really safe. Simple things that other people don’t think twice about, I start feeling like if I let my guard down or trust them, they’ll turn on me somehow. And I can tell myself, “That doesn’t make sense,” but it still sits there in my mind.\n\nIt’s tiring, because it makes the day feel… uneasy, I guess. Like I’m always waiting for something ordinary to become a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical sensations, like pain or tension, when you're feeling uneasy or on guard?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s hard for me to separate what’s in my body from what’s in my head, if that makes sense. When I’m uneasy, I don’t just think about things — I start checking them.\n\nLike if I go somewhere now, even a regular place, I catch myself looking around before I can relax. I look up at things, wondering what might fall. I look for the exits, where I’d go if I had to get out. And I pay attention to the floor, or the steps, whether they look steady, whether there’s something loose or uneven there.\n\nI don’t always mean to do it. It’s just like my body won’t let me sit easy until I’ve looked everything over. Then maybe I can settle a little, but even then I’m still sort of watching.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places or activities because of these feelings of unease?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think I do. I don’t always call it avoiding, because I’ll make it sound more reasonable in my own head, you know? I’ll tell myself I’m just tired, or the weather’s not right, or I’ve got something else to do around the house.\n\nBut I keep finding reasons not to go down to the senior center. I used to go more, just to be around people a bit, sit and talk, maybe have coffee. Now I’ll get ready like I might go, and then I start thinking about it too much, and pretty soon I’ve talked myself out of it.\n\nAnd the garage is another one. I don’t like being out there the way I used to. Especially around the tools… and over near where I used to keep that ladder. I’ll say, “Well, that can wait,” or “I don’t need to fool with that today,” but truthfully, I just don’t want to be near it. I can feel myself getting uneasy before I even get started, so I just shut the door and leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to repeat certain actions or rituals to help you feel safer or less uneasy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, maybe.\n\nI mean, I’ll check things, like I said before, and sometimes I might look at something more than once because I don’t quite believe myself the first time. But I don’t have, like, a set thing I do over and over, if that’s what you’re asking. No counting or touching things a certain number of times, nothing like that.\n\nIt’s more that I hesitate. I’ll stand there longer than I probably need to, looking something over, trying to feel sure. But I don’t know if I’d call that a ritual. It doesn’t feel organized like that. It’s just me trying to settle myself down, I guess, and sometimes it works a little, sometimes it doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific objects or situations that consistently make you feel uneasy?\n\nPatient: Yes… there are. I guess the two that come to mind right away are the senior center and my garage.\n\nThe senior center sounds harmless when I say it out loud, and I know that. It’s just a place where people sit around, talk, have coffee, maybe play cards. But I keep finding reasons not to go. I’ll think, “Well, maybe tomorrow,” or “I don’t really need to be out today,” and then the day passes and I haven’t gone. It’s not that anybody there has done anything wrong. It’s more the thought of going there and being out somewhere… I start getting uneasy before I even leave.\n\nAnd the garage, that one is more direct, I suppose. I don’t like being around the tools much anymore, and especially not over by the place where I used to keep the ladder. Even if I’m only going in there for something small, I notice myself staying away from that side. I’ll tell myself the work can wait, or I don’t have the right energy for it, but really I just don’t want to be near it. It sets me off inside some, so I shut the door and leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that repeat over and over in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that happens. Not all day every day, maybe, but when something sets it off, it’s hard to get it out of my head.\n\nLike if I hear metal scrape against something — even something simple, like somebody dragging a tool or a chair leg catching wrong — it can just… it jumps me right back. Or if I’m going past a house and there’s a ladder leaning up in the yard, not even my ladder, just any ladder sitting there at the wrong angle, I’ll see it and then my mind goes right back to it.\n\nIt’s like for a second I’m under there again, waiting for help. I know I’m not, I know I’m standing right where I am, but my head doesn’t seem to care right away. The picture comes back, and then I keep thinking about it even after I try to push it aside. I’ll tell myself, “Quit it, that’s over, you’re fine,” but it keeps circling around for a while. Sometimes I have to really work to get my mind onto something else.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that feel like they're forcing their way into your mind, even when you don't want to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s a good way to put it, I think. It’s not like I sit down and decide to think about it. I’ll be doing something ordinary, or just going along, and then if I hear metal scrape — like a tool dragging or something catching that sharp way — it just pushes right in.\n\nOr if I see a ladder leaning in somebody’s yard, even if it’s not mine and I don’t know the person, my mind doesn’t just notice it and move on. The whole thing comes back in my head. For a moment it’s like I’m under there again, waiting for help, and I don’t want to be thinking about that at all.\n\nI try to tell myself, “You’re not there now,” but the thought kind of forces its way in before I can stop it. It’s not constant, but when it happens, it really does feel like it gets into my head without permission.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts that come into your mind are a part of you or do they feel like they are coming from outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: No… I wouldn’t say it feels like it’s coming from outside me, like somebody else is putting it there. It’s in my own head, I know that.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel like I’m choosing it, either. That’s the part that bothers me. I’ll hear that metal scraping sound, or I’ll see a ladder leaning up in somebody’s yard, and before I even have time to think, the whole thing jumps back in my mind. It’s like I’m right back under there again, waiting for help.\n\nSo… it’s mine, I guess, but it doesn’t feel under my control. It just comes rushing in.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your unease or these repeating thoughts are affecting your ability to work or interact with family and friends?\n\nPatient: Yes… some. I mean, I’m not falling apart completely or anything. I still get up, wash up, eat, do the basic things I need to do. I can get through the day, mostly. But my day is a lot smaller than it used to be, if that makes sense.\n\nI don’t go to the senior center anymore like I used to. I used to go just to be around people, maybe play cards, talk a little, have coffee. Now I find reasons not to go. And around the house, there are things I just avoid, more than I like to admit. My wife ends up doing chores I used to handle, and I feel bad about that. I’ll tell myself I’m going to do it, then I see something or I start thinking too much, and I just leave it.\n\nAnd my bridge friends… I don’t visit with them much anymore either. I used to enjoy that. Now it feels easier to stay home, but then staying home makes everything feel even narrower. So yes, it has affected things. Not in a way where I can’t function at all, but enough that my wife notices, and I notice too.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your unease or these thoughts have been affecting your relationships with your family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I’d say it has, though maybe not in some big dramatic way. I still do my basic things, you know — I get up, I wash, I eat, I can manage the ordinary routine. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… that’s exactly how it feels. I can be thinking about something plain, like what I’m going to eat or whether I need to take the trash out, and then if I hear metal scrape somewhere, it just cuts right through whatever I was thinking.

Or if I’m riding by and see a ladder leaning up in somebody’s yard, even if it has nothing to do with me, the whole thing jumps back in my head before I can stop it. It’s not like I sit there and decide to remember it. It just comes in, and for a moment it feels like I’m under there again, waiting for help.

So yes, I’d call it intrusive. It comes whether I want it to or not.

When I’m uneasy, I don’t just think about things — I start checking them.\n\nLike if I go somewhere now, even a regular place, I catch myself looking around before I can relax. I look up at things, wondering what might fall. I look for the exits, where I’d go if I had to get out. And I pay attention to the floor, or the steps, whether they look steady, whether there’s something loose or uneven there.\n\nI don’t always mean to do it. It’s just like my body won’t let me sit easy until I’ve looked everything over. Then maybe I can settle a little, but even then I’m still sort of watching.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places or activities because of these feelings of unease?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think I do. I don’t always call it avoiding, because I’ll make it sound more reasonable in my own head, you know? I’ll tell myself I’m just tired, or the weather’s not right, or I’ve got something else to do around the house.\n\nBut I keep finding reasons not to go down to the senior center. I used to go more, just to be around people a bit, sit and talk, maybe have coffee. Now I’ll get ready like I might go, and then I start thinking about it too much, and pretty soon I’ve talked myself out of it.\n\nAnd the garage is another one. I don’t like being out there the way I used to. Especially around the tools… and over near where I used to keep that ladder. I’ll say, “Well, that can wait,” or “I don’t need to fool with that today,” but truthfully, I just don’t want to be near it. I can feel myself getting uneasy before I even get started, so I just shut the door and leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to repeat certain actions or rituals to help you feel safer or less uneasy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, maybe.\n\nI mean, I’ll check things, like I said before, and sometimes I might look at something more than once because I don’t quite believe myself the first time. But I don’t have, like, a set thing I do over and over, if that’s what you’re asking. No counting or touching things a certain number of times, nothing like that.\n\nIt’s more that I hesitate. I’ll stand there longer than I probably need to, looking something over, trying to feel sure. But I don’t know if I’d call that a ritual. It doesn’t feel organized like that. It’s just me trying to settle myself down, I guess, and sometimes it works a little, sometimes it doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific objects or situations that consistently make you feel uneasy?\n\nPatient: Yes… there are. I guess the two that come to mind right away are the senior center and my garage.\n\nThe senior center sounds harmless when I say it out loud, and I know that. It’s just a place where people sit around, talk, have coffee, maybe play cards. But I keep finding reasons not to go. I’ll think, “Well, maybe tomorrow,” or “I don’t really need to be out today,” and then the day passes and I haven’t gone. It’s not that anybody there has done anything wrong. It’s more the thought of going there and being out somewhere… I start getting uneasy before I even leave.\n\nAnd the garage, that one is more direct, I suppose. I don’t like being around the tools much anymore, and especially not over by the place where I used to keep the ladder. Even if I’m only going in there for something small, I notice myself staying away from that side. I’ll tell myself the work can wait, or I don’t have the right energy for it, but really I just don’t want to be near it. It sets me off inside some, so I shut the door and leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that repeat over and over in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that happens. Not all day every day, maybe, but when something sets it off, it’s hard to get it out of my head.\n\nLike if I hear metal scrape against something — even something simple, like somebody dragging a tool or a chair leg catching wrong — it can just… it jumps me right back. Or if I’m going past a house and there’s a ladder leaning up in the yard, not even my ladder, just any ladder sitting there at the wrong angle, I’ll see it and then my mind goes right back to it.\n\nIt’s like for a second I’m under there again, waiting for help. I know I’m not, I know I’m standing right where I am, but my head doesn’t seem to care right away. The picture comes back, and then I keep thinking about it even after I try to push it aside. I’ll tell myself, “Quit it, that’s over, you’re fine,” but it keeps circling around for a while. Sometimes I have to really work to get my mind onto something else.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that feel like they're forcing their way into your mind, even when you don't want to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s a good way to put it, I think. It’s not like I sit down and decide to think about it. I’ll be doing something ordinary, or just going along, and then if I hear metal scrape — like a tool dragging or something catching that sharp way — it just pushes right in.\n\nOr if I see a ladder leaning in somebody’s yard, even if it’s not mine and I don’t know the person, my mind doesn’t just notice it and move on. The whole thing comes back in my head. For a moment it’s like I’m under there again, waiting for help, and I don’t want to be thinking about that at all.\n\nI try to tell myself, “You’re not there now,” but the thought kind of forces its way in before I can stop it. It’s not constant, but when it happens, it really does feel like it gets into my head without permission.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts that come into your mind are a part of you or do they feel like they are coming from outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: No… I wouldn’t say it feels like it’s coming from outside me, like somebody else is putting it there. It’s in my own head, I know that.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel like I’m choosing it, either. That’s the part that bothers me. I’ll hear that metal scraping sound, or I’ll see a ladder leaning up in somebody’s yard, and before I even have time to think, the whole thing jumps back in my mind. It’s like I’m right back under there again, waiting for help.\n\nSo… it’s mine, I guess, but it doesn’t feel under my control. It just comes rushing in.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your unease or these repeating thoughts are affecting your ability to work or interact with family and friends?\n\nPatient: Yes… some. I mean, I’m not falling apart completely or anything. I still get up, wash up, eat, do the basic things I need to do. I can get through the day, mostly. But my day is a lot smaller than it used to be, if that makes sense.\n\nI don’t go to the senior center anymore like I used to. I used to go just to be around people, maybe play cards, talk a little, have coffee. Now I find reasons not to go. And around the house, there are things I just avoid, more than I like to admit. My wife ends up doing chores I used to handle, and I feel bad about that. I’ll tell myself I’m going to do it, then I see something or I start thinking too much, and I just leave it.\n\nAnd my bridge friends… I don’t visit with them much anymore either. I used to enjoy that. Now it feels easier to stay home, but then staying home makes everything feel even narrower. So yes, it has affected things. Not in a way where I can’t function at all, but enough that my wife notices, and I notice too.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your unease or these thoughts have been affecting your relationships with your family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I’d say it has, though maybe not in some big dramatic way. I still do my basic things, you know — I get up, I wash, I eat, I can manage the ordinary routine. But with people… I’ve pulled back more than I meant to.\n\nI don’t go to the senior center anymore, and I used to go there pretty regularly. It was a place to see folks, have coffee, sit and talk. Now I just don’t go. I tell myself I’m not in the mood, or I’ll go next week, but then next week comes and I still stay home.\n\nAnd with my wife, it affects things around the house. I avoid certain things, or I put them off so long that she ends up doing chores I used to take care of. I don’t like that. It makes me feel like I’m leaving things on her, and that’s not how I want to be.\n\nMy bridge friends too… I don’t visit with them much anymore. I used to enjoy that, just sitting with them and playing and talking. Now my days have gotten smaller, I guess. More of my time is just at home, not seeing people the way I used to. So yes, it has affected my relationships some.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts are intrusive, meaning they come into your mind even when you're trying to think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s exactly how it feels. I can be thinking about something plain, like what I’m going to eat or whether I need to take the trash out, and then if I hear metal scrape somewhere, it just cuts right through whatever I was thinking.\n\nOr if I’m riding by and see a ladder leaning up in somebody’s yard, even if it has nothing to do with me, the whole thing jumps back in my head before I can stop it. It’s not like I sit there and decide to remember it. It just comes in, and for a moment it feels like I’m under there again, waiting for help.\n\nSo yes, I’d call it intrusive. It comes whether I want it to or not.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think so. I still do a couple of the things I used to like, so it’s not like I’ve stopped everything altogether. I still go out and water the garden, and I’ll fuss with my old radios sometimes, take the back off one and look at the wires, clean a knob, that sort of thing.

But the feeling I used to get from it… that’s what’s changed. Before, watering the garden felt peaceful to me. I’d stand there and look at the plants, notice what was coming up, feel good that I was taking care of something. Now I do it more because it needs doing. Same with the radios. I can still sit there and tinker, but that little enjoyment, that satisfaction when something works again, it doesn’t come like it used to.

It’s hard to explain. I’m doing the motions, but the pleasure part is kind of dulled down. I keep hoping if I keep at it, maybe it’ll feel the way it used to, but most days it just doesn’t.
