The whole thing comes back in my head. For a moment it’s like I’m under there again, waiting for help, and I don’t want to be thinking about that at all.\n\nI try to tell myself, “You’re not there now,” but the thought kind of forces its way in before I can stop it. It’s not constant, but when it happens, it really does feel like it gets into my head without permission.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts that come into your mind are a part of you or do they feel like they are coming from outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: No… I wouldn’t say it feels like it’s coming from outside me, like somebody else is putting it there. It’s in my own head, I know that.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel like I’m choosing it, either. That’s the part that bothers me. I’ll hear that metal scraping sound, or I’ll see a ladder leaning up in somebody’s yard, and before I even have time to think, the whole thing jumps back in my mind. It’s like I’m right back under there again, waiting for help.\n\nSo… it’s mine, I guess, but it doesn’t feel under my control. It just comes rushing in.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your unease or these repeating thoughts are affecting your ability to work or interact with family and friends?\n\nPatient: Yes… some. I mean, I’m not falling apart completely or anything. I still get up, wash up, eat, do the basic things I need to do. I can get through the day, mostly. But my day is a lot smaller than it used to be, if that makes sense.\n\nI don’t go to the senior center anymore like I used to. I used to go just to be around people, maybe play cards, talk a little, have coffee. Now I find reasons not to go. And around the house, there are things I just avoid, more than I like to admit. My wife ends up doing chores I used to handle, and I feel bad about that. I’ll tell myself I’m going to do it, then I see something or I start thinking too much, and I just leave it.\n\nAnd my bridge friends… I don’t visit with them much anymore either. I used to enjoy that. Now it feels easier to stay home, but then staying home makes everything feel even narrower. So yes, it has affected things. Not in a way where I can’t function at all, but enough that my wife notices, and I notice too.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your unease or these thoughts have been affecting your relationships with your family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I’d say it has, though maybe not in some big dramatic way. I still do my basic things, you know — I get up, I wash, I eat, I can manage the ordinary routine. But with people… I’ve pulled back more than I meant to.\n\nI don’t go to the senior center anymore, and I used to go there pretty regularly. It was a place to see folks, have coffee, sit and talk. Now I just don’t go. I tell myself I’m not in the mood, or I’ll go next week, but then next week comes and I still stay home.\n\nAnd with my wife, it affects things around the house. I avoid certain things, or I put them off so long that she ends up doing chores I used to take care of. I don’t like that. It makes me feel like I’m leaving things on her, and that’s not how I want to be.\n\nMy bridge friends too… I don’t visit with them much anymore. I used to enjoy that, just sitting with them and playing and talking. Now my days have gotten smaller, I guess. More of my time is just at home, not seeing people the way I used to. So yes, it has affected my relationships some.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts are intrusive, meaning they come into your mind even when you're trying to think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s exactly how it feels. I can be thinking about something plain, like what I’m going to eat or whether I need to take the trash out, and then if I hear metal scrape somewhere, it just cuts right through whatever I was thinking.\n\nOr if I’m riding by and see a ladder leaning up in somebody’s yard, even if it has nothing to do with me, the whole thing jumps back in my head before I can stop it. It’s not like I sit there and decide to remember it. It just comes in, and for a moment it feels like I’m under there again, waiting for help.\n\nSo yes, I’d call it intrusive. It comes whether I want it to or not.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your unease or these thoughts have been affecting your ability to enjoy activities or hobbies that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I still do a couple of the things I used to like, so it’s not like I’ve stopped everything altogether. I still go out and water the garden, and I’ll fuss with my old radios sometimes, take the back off one and look at the wires, clean a knob, that sort of thing.\n\nBut the feeling I used to get from it… that’s what’s changed. Before, watering the garden felt peaceful to me. I’d stand there and look at the plants, notice what was coming up, feel good that I was taking care of something. Now I do it more because it needs doing. Same with the radios. I can still sit there and tinker, but that little enjoyment, that satisfaction when something works again, it doesn’t come like it used to.\n\nIt’s hard to explain. I’m doing the motions, but the pleasure part is kind of dulled down. I keep hoping if I keep at it, maybe it’ll feel the way it used to, but most days it just doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do certain things in a specific way to feel safe or to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I don’t know if I’d call it a ritual exactly. It’s more that when I go somewhere now, I can’t just walk in and sit down like I used to.\n\nI find myself looking around first — up above me, at shelves or fixtures or anything that looks like it could come loose or fall. Then I notice where the exits are, even if it’s just a little room or somebody’s house. I look at the floor too, or the steps, to see if they seem steady, if there’s anything uneven or shaky. I’ll stand there a moment longer than I need to, just checking it all with my eyes.\n\nIt’s not that I think doing that will magically stop something bad, but I don’t feel settled until I’ve looked. Once I know where things are and that nothing looks ready to give way, then I can sit a little easier. If I don’t do it, I stay tense, like I missed something important.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these intrusive thoughts are a part of who you are, or do they feel alien to you?\n\nPatient: No… I wouldn’t say they feel like they’re part of who I am. They feel more like something that barges in on me.\n\nI mean, I know it’s my mind having the thought, but it doesn’t feel like me choosing it, if that makes sense. I’ll be going along, just minding my business, and then I hear metal scrape somewhere, or I see a ladder leaning in somebody’s yard, and all at once the whole thing jumps back into my head. It’s not like an ordinary memory where you sit and think, “Oh, I remember that.” It comes in hard and sudden, and for a bit it’s like I’m under there again, waiting for help.\n\nThat doesn’t feel like me. It feels alien, I guess is the word you used. It feels like something that got stuck in me and keeps coming back up when I don’t want it to.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your symptoms have been impacting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I can still get through my day, mostly. I mean, I get up, wash, eat, do the regular things a man has to do. So it’s not like I’m helpless or anything like that. But my life has gotten… smaller, I guess that’s the best way to say it.\n\nI used to go down to the senior center, not every day, but often enough. I’d sit, talk with people, maybe play a game, have coffee. I don’t do that much anymore. I keep telling myself I’ll go next week, or when I’m feeling more settled, but then I just don’t. Same with my bridge friends. I used to visit with them, and that gave some shape to the week. Now I hardly see them. I miss it, but at the same time I don’t push myself to go.\n\nAnd around the house, I still do what I can, but there are things I avoid now. Little chores I used to handle without thinking, my wife ends up doing them or reminding me about them. I don’t like that. I’ve always tried to be useful around the house, and it bothers me seeing her take on things that used to be mine.\n\nSo yes, overall, I’d say it’s affected the quality of my life. I’m still managing, but I’m not living the same way I was. My days feel more closed in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repetitive thoughts or urges to check things are focused on preventing a specific bad thing from happening, like an accident or harm to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is aimed at that, in a way. Not like I believe I can control everything, but it’s mostly about making sure something isn’t going to fall, or that I’m not going to get caught somewhere if something gives way.\n\nWhen I go places now, I don’t just sit down. I look around first. I look up at anything overhead, shelves, lights, whatever might come loose. I check where the exits are, even if it’s just a small room. And I look at the floor or the steps, whether they seem steady or uneven. I need to see those things before I can feel at ease.\n\nSo yes, it’s specific in that sense. I’m checking for danger, I suppose — things falling, a way out, whether where I’m standing is safe. If I don’t look, I feel like I’ve left myself open to something happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel extremely tired or lacking in energy, even after getting enough rest?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “extremely” tired, no. I mean, I’m 74, so I don’t expect to feel like I did when I was a young man, and some days I move slower than others. That feels pretty ordinary to me.\n\nIf I’ve had a busy day, or I’ve been out running errands, then sure, I’ll sit down and feel worn out. But I haven’t really noticed that I’m exhausted even after resting, not in a way that stands out to me. Most mornings, if I’ve slept enough, I can get up and do the usual things. Maybe not with a spring in my step, but I can manage.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think that’s been one of the main problems for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your symptoms are significantly interfering with your daily life or causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes, it does interfere, though I wouldn’t say I’m falling apart or anything like that. I still manage my basic routine. I get up, get washed, eat, take care of the ordinary things a person has to do in a day. So from the outside maybe it doesn’t look so serious.\n\nBut it has changed my life. I don’t go to the senior center much anymore, and that used to be part of my week. I’d see people, talk a little, have coffee, just be around others. Now I keep putting it off. And I don’t visit with my bridge friends the way I used to either, so there are fewer things to look forward to.\n\nAround the house, too, I avoid certain things now, enough that my wife ends up doing chores I used to handle myself. That bothers me. I don’t like feeling like I’ve handed things over to her when I should still be able to do them.\n\nSo yes… it’s not that I can’t function at all, but my days have gotten smaller. And that is distressing, because I notice it. I know I’m not living the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform any specific rituals or behaviors in order to reduce your anxiety or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I don’t think of it like a ritual exactly. It’s more like something I have to do before I can settle myself.\n\nWhen I go somewhere now, I can’t just walk in and sit down like I used to. I’ll look around first — not in a dramatic way, but I’m checking. I look up to see if there’s anything overhead that looks like it could fall, like shelves or lights or whatever’s hanging there. Then I look for where the exits are, even if it’s a place I’ve been before. And I pay attention to the floor or the steps, whether they look steady, whether there’s anything uneven.\n\nIf I don’t do that, I feel on edge, like I’m sitting there without knowing whether I’m safe. Once I’ve looked, I can breathe a little easier and sit down, but it’s not really a choice anymore. It’s become part of how I enter a place."
